The encoder is depicted as a light blue rectangle with the text 'The house is red' below it, indicating it processes the input sequence. Sequence to Sequence Generation with Transformer Based Models•Input: Sequence x1, …, xT •Output: Sequence y1, …, yT’•Transformer is an encoder-decoder architecture:•An encoder (neural network) analyzes the input and builds an intermediate representation (a.k.a. context vector) of it; •A decoder (neural network) analyzes the intermediate representation and creates an output.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The diagram illustrates the process of language translation using transformers, divided into two main steps. Step 1 shows a sequence of encoder blocks that receive input tokens such as 'x1=I', 'x2=want', 'x3=to', 'x4=buy', 'x5=a', 'x6=car', and 'x7=EOS'. Each input token is represented by a green bar, and the output from the last encoder block is an embedding vector 'X1:7'. Step 2 depicts the autoregressive generation process where the embedding vector 'X1:7' is passed through a series of decoder blocks. The first decoder block takes the embedding vector 'X1:7' as input and outputs 'y0=BOS', which is then used as the initial input for the second decoder block to produce 'y1=Ich'. The decoder blocks are represented by red rectangles, and the arrows indicate the flow of information from the embedding vector through the blocks. The diagram is labeled 'Example: Language Translation with Transformers' and includes a source reference at the bottom.]

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a diagram illustrating the concept of 'Positional Encoding' in the context of machine learning or artificial intelligence. At the top of the diagram, there are three boxes labeled y0, y1, y2, representing input values. These are connected to a box labeled 'self-attention' which suggests a process where each input value is considered in relation to the others. Below the self-attention box, there are three boxes labeled x0, x1, x2, and p0, p1, p2, representing input vectors and positional encodings, respectively. These are connected to a box labeled 'position encoding' which indicates that a unique positional encoding is concatenated to each input vector. The diagram then shows that a function pos: N →Rd is used to process the position of the vector into a d-dimensional vector, and the result is denoted as pj = pos(j). On the right side of the diagram, there are four numbered points outlining the desirability of the function pos(.), such as outputting a unique encoding for each time-step, consistent distance between time-steps, generalization to longer sentences, and being deterministic. The source of the information is credited to 'Attention is all you need,' NeuralIPS, 2017]

Positional Encoding
19
 Source: “Attention is all you need,”NeurIPS,2017.
Desiderata of pos(.) : 1.It should output a unique encoding for each time-step (word’s position in a sentence)2.Distance between any two time-steps should be consistent across sentences with different lengths.3.Our model should generalize to longer sentences without any efforts. Its values should be bounded.4.It must be deterministic.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The diagram presents the concept of Positional Encoding in a neural network model. It is divided into three main sections. The first section shows a sequence of inputs labeled 'x0, x1, x2' and their corresponding outputs 'p0, p1, p2' after a 'self-attention' process, which leads to the final output sequence 'y0, y1, y2'. The second section, labeled 'position encoding', indicates that a special positional encoding 'pj' is concatenated to each input vector 'xj'. The third section, titled 'Desiderata of pos(.)', lists four requirements for the positional encoding function: it should output a unique encoding for each time-step, maintain consistent distance between time-steps across sentences of different lengths, generalize to longer sentences without additional efforts, and be deterministic. 20
 Source: “Attention is all you need,”NeurIPS,2017.
Desiderata of pos(.) : 1.It should output a unique encoding for each time-step (word’s position in a sentence)2.Distance between any two time-steps should be consistent across sentences with different lengths.3.Our model should generalize to longer sentences without any efforts. Its values should be bounded.4.It must be deterministic.
Options for pos(.)1. Learn a lookup table:       ○ Learn parameters to use for pos(t) for t ∈[0, T)       ○ Lookup table contains T x d parameters.

Self-Attention Layer
26 Source: https://cs231n.stanford.edu/slides/2022/lecture_11_ruohan.pdf
Inputs: Input vectors: x (shape: N x D)
Operations: Query vectors: q = xWq Key vectors: k = xWk Value vectors: v = xWv Alignment: ei,j = qj ᐧki / √D Attention: a = softmax(e) Output: yj = ∑i ai,j ᐧvi
Outputs: Context vectors: y (shape: N x D)

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The slide is titled 'Denoising Diffusion Models' and is the 41st slide in a presentation, as indicated by the page number at the bottom. The slide explains that denoising diffusion models consist of two processes: a forward diffusion process that gradually adds noise to input and a reverse denoising process that learns to generate data by denoising. The slide includes a visual representation of these processes. On the left side, there is an image of a cat that progressively becomes more distorted and noisy as it moves from left to right, representing the forward diffusion process. The right side shows the same sequence of images but labeled 'Noise,' indicating the final outcome after the noise has been fully added. Below this sequence, there is a reverse sequence where the images gradually recover from the noise, showing the reverse denoising process. Observations                             [Google Research 2023]
52
nOpen-web datasets used to train open text-to-image models suffer from significant issues.lTo be specific, the captions in the LAION dataset, used to train vision-language models, come from alt HTML tags (Alttext) and often describes only a narrow aspect of the image, neglecting significant visual details.lFor example, an image of a person can have as Alttext the name of the person and the name of the photographer, but not a description of their appearance, their clothes, their position, or the background.
Source: “A Picture is Worth a Thousand Words: Principled Recaptioning Improves Image Generation,” arXiv, 2023.

The RECAP Framework 
55
nThis work proposes to relabel the corpus with improved captions that are auto-generated by a custom I2T (image-to-text) model, so as to increase sample efficiency and allow a vision-language model to better understand the relations between captions and images.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a presentation slide titled 'Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools' for NeurIPS 2023. It outlines a process for language models to generate new text based on given input text by interleaving API calls. The slide is divided into three main sections, each with a numbered step and a corresponding flow diagram. The first section has a pink background and includes the title '1 Sample API Calls' with examples of API calls such as 'What other name is Pittsburgh known by?' and 'Which country is Pittsburgh in?' The second section is blue and titled '2 Execute API Calls' with examples 'Steel City' and 'United States.' The third section is again pink and titled '3 Filter API Calls' with examples 'Pittsburgh is also known as Steel City' and 'the Steel City. the Steel City.' The process results in a new text 'X*' in the final step. The slide also includes the text '73' at the bottom.]

Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools       [NeurIPS 2023]Key steps: Given an input text x, we first sample a position i and corresponding API call candidates c1i , c2i , . . . , cki. We then execute these API calls and filter out all calls which do not reduce the loss Li over the next tokens. All remaining API calls are interleaved with the original text, resulting in a new text x∗.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The slide presents information about 'Reflexion' as a concept in the context of NeuroIPS 2023. The slide has a title at the top, a main body of text, and a source citation at the bottom. The title is 'Reflexion' with a reference to NeuroIPS 2023 in brackets. Below the title, there is a paragraph explaining the concept of Reflexion, which uses verbal reinforcement to help agents learn from prior failures by converting binary or scalar feedback into verbal feedback in the form of a textual summary. This summary is then provided as additional context for the MLLM agent in the next episode. The slide lists three distinct models utilized by Reflexion: Actor, Evaluator, and Self-Reflection model. The Actor is responsible for generating text and actions, the Evaluator scores the outputs produced by the Actor, and the Self-Reflection model generates verbal reinforcement cues to assist the Actor in self-improvement. The source of the information is cited at the bottom as 'Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning,' from NeuroIPS, 2023. The slide number is indicated as 79.]

Reflexion [NeurIPS 2023]Reflexion uses verbal reinforcement to help agents learn from prior failings, i.e., converting binary (e.g., success/fail) or scalar feedback from the environment into verbal feedback in the form of a textual summary, which is then added as additional context for the MLLM agent in the next episode.Reflexion utilizes three distinct models:•Actor: generating text and actions•Evaluator: scoring the outputs produced by the Actor•Self-Reflection model: generating verbal reinforcement cues to assist the Actor in self-improvement.79 Source: “Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning,” NeurIPS, 2023.

The Reflexion process•Initialization•The Actor produces a trajectory "" by interacting with the environment.•The Evaluator then produces a score #"	which is computed as "#=&$(""), where "# is only a scalar reward for trial ).•The Self-Reflection model analyzes the set of {"", #"} to produce a summary *#" which is stored in the memory +,+, where *## is a verbal experience feedback for trial ). •Repeat•The Actor, Evaluator, and Self-Reflection model work together through trials in a loop until the Evaluator deems *## to be correct. Note that after each trial ), *##, is appended +,+. 80

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The diagram illustrates the Reflexion process as part of artificial intelligence. At the top, the title 'The Reflexion process' is prominently displayed. The process begins with 'Agent' and is divided into two main memory systems: 'Internal feedback' and 'Experience (long-term memory)'. How to perform step by step reasoning without using demonstration examples?Analogical PromptingThe inspiration comes from analogical reasoning in psychology, a concept where humans draw from relevant past experiences to tackle new problems.For instance, when faced with a new math problem, humans often think about “do I know a related problem?” and recall how they solved related problems in the past to derive insights for solving the new problem.20 Source: “Large Language Models as Analogical Reasoners,” ICLR, 2024.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image contains three separate diagrams illustrating different approaches to inference in AI models. Each diagram is labeled with a specific type of search method: Best-of-N Search, Sentence-level Beam Search, and Stage-level Beam Search. The diagrams are structured to show the process of generating responses or options based on a given question. In the Best-of-N Search, a single response is generated and selected. In the Sentence-level Beam Search, multiple responses are generated for each sentence and the best one is chosen. In the Stage-level Beam Search, responses are generated for each reasoning stage, such as summary, caption, reasoning, and conclusion, and the best option is selected at each stage. The diagrams use a combination of red, green, yellow, and blue colors to represent different outcomes: red for rejected upon verification, green for selected upon verification, yellow for skipped verification, and blue for applying verification. The source of the information is cited as 'LLaVA-CoT: Let Vision Language Models Reason Step-by-Step,' arXiv, 2025.]

Inference Approaches•Best-of-N search generates N complete responses and selects the best one among them.•Sentence-level Beam Searchgenerates multiple candidate options for each sentence and chooses the best one. •Stage-level Beam Searchgenerates candidates for each reasoning stage (e.g., summary, caption, reasoning, and conclusion) and selects the best option at each stage.
31 Source: “LLaVA-CoT: Let Vision Language Models Reason Step-by-Step,” arXiv, 2025.

It contains two bullet points of text explaining that Chain-of-Thought (CoT) guides mathematical logical reasoning (MLLM) step-by-step but is not conducive to creative problem-solving which requires out-of-box thoughts and is crucial for innovation advancements. Leap-of-Thought (LoT)•CoTguides MLLM to reason step-by-step, and can motivate their logical reasoning ability. While effective for logical tasks, CoTis not conducive to creative problem-solving which often requires out-of-box thoughts and is crucial for innovation advancements.•CoTis a non-sequential, creative paradigm involving strong associations and knowledge leaps.

Image Declaration•Firstly, we allocate a unique image proxy ([IMGj]) to reference the visual embedding of image j, which provides a unique identifier for MLLMs to index and distinguish between visual and text embeddings. •Then, we utilize natural language prompts to establish references between text and image. •Incorporating the explicit text-to-image reference in the image declaration assists the model in correlating the text with the appropriate image.45 Source: “MMICL: Empowering Vision-language Model with Multi-Modal In-Context Learning,” ICLR, 2024.

Multimodal CoTReasoning•Multimodal-CoTis a proposed mechanism to incorporate language (text) and vision (images) modalities into a two-stage framework that separates rationale generation and answer inference.•Both stages share the same model structure but differ in the input and output.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The diagram presents two paradigms to achieve Multimodal CoT, which is Chain-of-Thought Reasoning. On the left side, labeled as (a) Caption-based Reasoning, there is a flow that starts with an 'Image' that feeds into a 'Caption Model'. This model then generates a 'Caption' which is then used to create 'Prompted Text'. This prompted text is input into an 'LLM' (Language Model), which then produces an 'Answer'. On the right side, labeled as (b) CoT-based Reasoning, the process starts with an 'Image' that goes into a 'Model', which produces a 'Rationale'. Both paradigms start with an 'Image' and result in an 'Answer', but the middle steps involving text and reasoning differ. Multimodal CoTReasoning•Error Analysis•The most prevalent error type is commonsense mistakes, accounting for 80% of the errors. These mistakes occur when the model is faced with questions that require commonsense knowledge, such as interpreting maps, counting objects in images, or utilizing the alphabet.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The diagram illustrates the concept of 'Dataset Distillation' in the context of deep learning. At the top, there is a large collection of 50K real training images, which are being fed into a process labeled 'Train' and then directed into 'Dataset Distillation'. This process results in a smaller collection of 10 synthetic training images. These synthetic images are then used in another training process, depicted with the same 'Train' label, leading to two similar neural network structures. These structures are shown at the bottom, with an arrow indicating that they have 'Similar Test Performance'. The diagram suggests that dataset distillation is a method to reduce data requirements in deep learning by condensing a large, real dataset into a smaller, synthetic one, while still achieving a similar level of performance in testing.]

Dataset Distillation•Dataset distillation has emerged as a powerful approach for reducing data requirements in deep learning•i.e., condensing a large, real dataset into a smaller, synthetic one

Distribution Matching (DM)•Among various methods, distribution matching (DM) based approaches stand out for their balance of computational efficiency and strong performance.•A key challenge in DM lies in defining an effective metric to measure distributional discrepancies between real and synthetic datasets.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a slide from a presentation discussing 'DM: feature point matching methods'. It highlights the use of Mean Squared Error (MSE) as a primary method for comparing point-wise features in Euclidean space. The slide includes a diagram and a mathematical formula. The diagram shows two datasets, labeled 'Synthetic Data' and 'Real Data', represented by red and blue circles respectively. These datasets are projected onto a Euclidean space, ZR, and the goal is to minimize the distance between these projections. The mathematical formula provided is: L_MSE = E_(x̃ ~ D̃)[ ||f(x) - f(x̃)||^2 ], where f denotes the feature extractor network, D and D̃ represent the real and synthetic data distributions respectively, and x̃ and x are samples drawn from D and D̃. The slide also includes a note on the previous paradigm: optimize D̃ to minimize the distance within Z.]

DM: feature point matching methods•Early methods primarily employed Mean Squared Error (MSE) to compare point-wise features, which operates in Euclidean space, ZR.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a presentation slide titled 'DM: moment matching methods'. It includes a brief text explanation stating that while distributional equivalence implies moment equivalence, the converse is not necessarily true, and aligning moments alone does not guarantee full distributional matching. The slide is divided into two main sections, each illustrating a different approach to minimizing the distance within a latent space Z. The first section, labeled 'MSE', shows a process of minimizing the loss L(D, D1|Z) where D1 is the synthetic data and D is the data from a parametrized network, aiming to align synthetic data moments to real data moments. The second section, labeled 'MMD', shows a similar process but with the loss function being L(D, D1|ZH) where ZH is another latent space, illustrating a method to minimize the distance between synthetic and real data moments within different latent spaces. The slide is visually supported with diagrams showing data points in the latent space, connected by dashed lines indicating the flow of data processing.]

DM: moment matching methods•Later methods employ Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD), aligning moments of distributions in a latent Hilbert space, ZH.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a plain white background with text consisting of bullet points and a mathematical equation. The bullet points are in black, and the mathematical equation is in a different font and aligned to the right side of the slide. The text explains a concept related to Adversarial Distribution Matching, involving a novel approach to distribution matching as an adversarial minmax optimization problem. The bullet points detail the steps of the approach, which includes maximizing a discrepancy measure to define a robust discrepancy metric, parameterized by a neural network. This metric is then used to minimize the discrepancy between the synthetic dataset and the real dataset by optimizing the synthetic data distribution. The mathematical equation represents this optimization problem, where L denotes the discrepancy measure, f is the feature extractor network, and ψ is the network learning the discrepancy metric. The equation is used to seek the optimal synthetic data distribution that minimizes the discrepancy while maximizing the metric. The slide number is indicated at the bottom right corner as '15'.]

Adversarial Distribution Matching•This paper proposes a novel approach that reformulates distribution matching as an adversarial minmax optimization problem.•We maximize the discrepancy measure to define a robust discrepancy metric, parameterized by a neural network ψ. •Concurrently, we minimize the discrepancy between the synthetic dataset D˜ and the real dataset D by optimizing the synthetic data distribution D˜. •Formally, this minmax optimization problem is expressed as:
•where L denotes the discrepancy measure, f is the feature extractor network, and ψ is the network learning the discrepancy metric. •This minmax framework seeks the optimal synthetic data distribution D˜ that minimizes L while network ψ maximizes L to establish a robust metric.15

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The chart is a circular graph divided into 360 degrees, representing the characteristic function (CF) matching process over iterations. The graph is labeled with angles from 0 degrees to 360 degrees, marked at every 30 degrees interval. There are three iterations displayed: 0, 5000, and 10000. For each iteration, there are two sets of data represented in different colors: blue for 'Real' and red for 'Synthetic'. The 'Real' data is a solid shape, while the 'Synthetic' data is composed of multiple smaller shapes that form a complex pattern. As the iterations progress from 0 to 10000, the red 'Synthetic' shapes expand and align more closely with the blue 'Real' shape, indicating that the characteristic function matching process is reducing the differences between the real and synthetic distributions. This suggests that the synthetic distribution is becoming more similar to the real distribution over time.]

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The diagram illustrates the process of Dataset Distillation with NCFM (Neural Characteristic Function Discrepancy). It shows two datasets, one real and one synthetic, both undergoing a feature extractor network to embed data points. The synthetic data is then optimized by minimizing the distributional discrepancy between the real and synthetic data, measured via the Neural Characteristic Function Discrepancy (NCFD) in the complex plane. There is also an auxiliary network that learns an optimal sampling distribution for the frequency arguments of the characteristic function. The complex plane is represented with a circular graph with angles and a radial axis, and vectors indicating the gradient of L for both synthetic and sampling datasets. The diagram includes various symbols and mathematical notations to represent the process, such as f(x), f̃(x), L, and the optimization process involving min L and max L with a theta function.]

Dataset Distillation with NCFM•Real and synthetic data points are sampled and embedded through a feature extractor network. The synthetic data is optimized by minimizing the distributional discrepancy between real and synthetic data, measured via the Neural Characteristic Function Discrepancy (NCFD) in the complex plane. Additionally, an auxiliary network learns an optimal sampling distribution for the frequency arguments of the characteristic function.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a slide titled 'Discounting' which explains the concept of discounting in a mathematical context. It is divided into three sections with bullet points and a diagram on the right side. The first section, 'How to discount?', states that each time we descend a level, we multiply in the discount once. The second section, 'Why discount?', explains that sooner rewards probably have higher utility than later rewards and helps algorithms converge. The third section, 'Example: discount of 0.5', provides a calculation example where U([1,2,3]) = 1*1 + 0.5*2 + 0.25*3 = 2.75 and U([3,2,1]) = 1*3 + 0.5*2 + 0.25*1 = 5.25, concluding that U([1,2,3]) < U([3,2,1]). The diagram on the right shows a sequence of steps with nodes connected by arrows, indicating a process flow. The nodes are represented by colored shapes such as circles, triangles, and diamonds, with arrows showing the direction of the process.]

Discounting■Howtodiscount?■Eachtimewe descendalevel,we multiplyinthediscountonce■Whydiscount?■Soonerrewardsprobablydohave higherutilitythanlaterrewards■Alsohelpsouralgorithmsconverge■Example:discountof0.5■U([1,2,3])=1*1+0.5*2+0.25*3=2.75■U([3,2,1])=1*3+0.5*2+0.25*1=5.25■U([1,2,3])<U([3,2,1])

It also mentions that this approach gives nonstationary policies, where policies depend on the time left. The second solution is not visible in the image. doc.page_content: [Image caption: Slide titled "Infinite Utilities" presents a problem and solutions related to infinite rewards in a game that lasts forever. It discusses a problem statement, two main solutions, and a mathematical equation. The first solution is "Finite horizon," likened to depth-limited search, with a sub-point explaining that it terminalizes episodes after a fixed number of steps and gives nonstationary policies. The second solution is "Discounting," with a condition that γ must be between 0 and 1, followed by a mathematical equation that describes the sum of discounted rewards. The equation is presented as a sum from t=0 to infinity, with the sum of γ raised to the power of t times Rmax divided by (1-γ). A sub-point under the equation states that smaller γ means a smaller "horizon" and a shorter term focus. The third solution is "Absorbing state," which guarantees that for every policy, a terminal state will eventually be reached, similar to an 'overheated' state in racing. On the right side of the slide, there is an image of a blue car navigating through a winding, brown path, which could be a metaphor for the journey through infinite utilities.]

■Smaller𝛾meanssmaller“horizon”–shortertermfocus■Absorbingstate:guarantee thatforeverypolicy,aterminalstatewilleventually bereached (like“overheated”forracing)

The bullet points also mention that this process is repeated until convergence, yielding V*. On the right side of the slide, there is a diagram illustrating the value iteration process. doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a presentation slide titled 'Q-Learning'. It explains the concept of Q-Learning, which is a sample-based Q-value iteration method. The slide includes a mathematical formula for Q-Learning, which is used to update the Q-values. The formula is: Qk+1(s, a) = Σ T(s, a, s') [ R(s, a, s') + γ max Qk(s', a') ], where Q(k+1)(s, a) is the updated Q-value, T(s, a, s') is the transition probability, R(s, a, s') is the reward, γ is the discount factor, and max Qk(s', a') is the maximum Q-value for the next state and action. The slide also outlines a four-step process to learn Q(s, a) values as you go: receiving a sample (s, a, s', r), considering the old estimate Q(s, a), considering the new sample estimate, and incorporating the new estimate into a running average: Q(s, a) = (1 - α)Q(s, a) + α [sample]. Additionally, there is a grid of Q-values shown in green, red, and gray squares, indicating the Q-values]

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a slide titled "Generalizing Across States" and contains text and illustrations. The text explains that basic Q-Learning keeps a table of all q-values, but in realistic situations, it is not possible to learn about every single state. It mentions that there are too many states to visit them all in training and too many states to hold the q-tables in memory. Instead, the slide suggests generalizing by learning about some small number of training states from experience and generalizing that experience to new, similar situations. This is a fundamental idea in machine learning, and the slide mentions that this concept will be seen again. There are two illustrations on the slide. The first one, on the top left, shows a robot reading a book with green and red puzzle pieces. The second illustration, on the bottom right, shows a robot in front of a bridge with a ghost character on the other side, and another robot below the bridge.]

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The image is a slide titled 'Approximate Q-Learning'. It explains how using a feature representation, denoted as f1, f2, ..., fN, a value function or value function, V(s) for any state s, can be written using weights w1, w2, ...wn. The formula for the value function is given as V(s) = w1f1(s) + w2f2(s) + ... + wnfn(s). Similarly, the formula for the value function for any state s, a, is Q(s, a) = w1f1(s, a) + w2f2(s, a) + ... + wnfn(s, a). The slide highlights the advantage of summarizing experience in a few powerful numbers and also points out the disadvantage where states may share features but are actually very different in value. An example is given showing two states that would have the same value if ghost positions are not included as a feature, illustrated with two images of a maze with a ghost and a different creature.]

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The diagram illustrates a simplified process of updating the Q-table in a neural network setting, specifically for training Deep Q-Networks (DQNs). It shows a mathematical equation that represents the update rule for the Q-value of a state-action pair. The equation is Q(s_t, a_t) <- Q(s_t, a_t) + α [r_t+1 + λmax Q(s_t+1, a) - Q(s_t, a_t)], where α represents the learning rate, r_t+1 is the reward received after taking action a_t in state s_t, and λmax is a discount factor applied to the maximum Q-value of the next state s_t+1 and any possible action a. The equation is broken down into parts, each highlighted with a different color and corresponding to a component of the original Q-learning algorithm. A note on the side explains that with the neural network replacing the Q-table, the learning rate is no longer needed because the backpropagating optimizer will take care of it. Additionally, it is mentioned that once the learning rate is removed, the two Q(s, a) terms can also be eliminated as they cancel each other out after removing the learning rate.]

Training DQNs
142
nWith the neural network taking the place of the Q-table, we can simplify the Q-learning algorithm.
•The learning rate is no longer needed, as our back-propagating optimizer will already have that.•Once the learning rate is removed, you realize that you can also remove the two Q(s, a) terms, as they cancel each other out after getting rid of the learning rate.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The slide is titled 'Challenges in Deep Reinforcement Learning' and is numbered 143. It features a mathematical equation that represents the challenge of having a non-stationary or unstable learning target in reinforcement learning. The equation is a reward function Q(s_t, a_t) which is composed of Q(s_t, a_t) plus an additional term 'α' times 'r_t+1' plus 'λ' times the maximum of Q(s_t+1, a), minus Q(s_t, a_t). Below the equation, there is an explanation that the learning target is continuously changing with each iteration, and in deep learning, the target variable does not change, making the training stable, which is not true for reinforcement learning. The slide text summarizes that in reinforcement learning, the policy or value functions that are often used to sample actions change as we continuously learn what to explore, leading to changes in the output as we get to know more about the ground truth values of states and actions.]

Challenges in Deep Reinforcement Learning
143
nNon-stationary or unstable learning target
nTo summarize, we often depend on the policy or value functions in reinforcement learning to sample actions. However, this is frequently changing as we continuously learn what to explore. As we play out the game, we get to know more about the ground truth values of states and actions and hence, the output is also changing.
The learning target is continuously changing with each iteration. In deep learning, the target variable does not change and hence the training is stable, which is just not true for reinforcement learning.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The slide contains text content with a title and bullet points discussing a concept in AI. The title reads 'Tricks for handling a constantly changing input and output' and is followed by a subheading 'Target Network'. There are three bullet points under this subheading explaining a technique related to neural networks. The first bullet point discusses the potential divergence between predicted and target values when using the same network for both, suggesting the use of two separate networks. The second bullet point describes a target network with a fixed architecture and parameters that are copied from the prediction network in every iteration. The third bullet point concludes that this method leads to more stable training. At the bottom right corner of the slide, there is a number '144' indicating the slide number in the presentation.]

Tricks for handling a constantly changing input and output 
144
nTarget NetworklSince the same network is calculating the predicted value and the target value, there could be a lot of divergence between these two. So, instead of using one neural network for learning, we can use two. lWe could use a separate network to estimate the target. This target network has the same architecture as the function approximator but with frozen parameters. For every C iterations (a hyperparameter), the parameters from the prediction network are copied to the target network. lThis leads to more stable training because it keeps the target function fixed (for a while).

Tricks for handling a constantly changing input and output 
146
nExperience ReplaylInstead of running Q-learning on state/action pairs as they occur during simulation or the actual experience, we ‘replay’ the stored data [state, action, reward, next_state].lFor example, suppose we are trying to build a video game bot where each frame of the game represents a different state. During training, we could sample a random batch of 64 frames from the last 100,000 frames to train our network.

A Case Study
147
nTask: Language Model Self-Improvement (LMSI)lTo improve language models without requiring external supervision, in an unsupervised training mannerlTwo notable LMSI works•RLCAI (arXiv 2022)•RLAIF (arXiv 2023)RLCAI employs a model to evaluate which of the two answers better adheres to a set of rules or principles, subsequently creating a dataset of AI preferences. The system then trains using reinforcement learning, with the preference model as the reward signal.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: Slide 158 titled 'BLEU (1/4)' discusses the method of computing unigrams precision for evaluating machine translation. It explains that to compute this precision, one must count how many words in a candidate text C are present in the reference text R, then divide the result by the number of words in C to get a percentage. The slide notes that a perfect score is 1.0, whereas the worst score is 0.0. An example is provided where the reference text R is 'The cat is on the mat.' and the candidate text C is 'The cat and the dog.' It is noted that in C, there are three words ('the', 'cat', 'the') that appear in R, resulting in a BLEU score of 0.6. The slide also addresses the problem with repeating unigrams, illustrating it with a candidate text C: 'The The The The' which, despite having a perfect 5/5 match in unigrams, results in a BLEU score of 1!, indicating a problem with the method when the same word is repeated excessively.]

158
nComputing unigrams precisionlTo count how many words in a candidate text C are present in the reference text R, and divide the result by the number of words in C to get a percentage. •Therefore, a perfect score is 1.0, whereas the worst score is 0.0.lExample:•R: The cat is on the mat. •C: The cat and the dog.•In C, there are three words (“the”, “cat”, “the”) that appear on the reference R, thus: BLEU*(C) = 3/5 = 0.6lThe problem with repeating unigrams•Consider C: “The The The The The”, BLEU*(C) = 5/5 = 1!
BLEU (1/4)
doc.metadata: {'producer': 'macOS 版本15.3.1（版號24D70） Quartz PDFContext, AppendMode 1.1', 'creator': 'PyPDF', 'creationdate': "D:20250405174744Z00'00'", 'moddate': "D:20250405174904Z00'00'", 'source': 'AI.pdf', 'total_pages': 463, 'page': 442, 'page_label': '443'}

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The slide is titled 'BLEU (2/4)' and discusses the concept of computing n-grams precision for evaluating the quality of text. It explains a method to achieve higher scores for well-formed sentences by considering matching 2-grams or 3-grams instead of just 1-grams. Two types of BLEU scores are introduced: BLEU**1, which considers only 1-grams, and BLEU**2, which considers only 2-grams.

An example is provided to illustrate the concept. The reference sentence (R) is 'There is a cat on the mat.' Two candidate sentences (C1 and C2) are compared against the reference. C1 is 'There is a cat on the mat.' which has six 2-grams that all appear in the reference, resulting in BLEU**1(C1) = 7/7 = 1.0 and BLEU**2(C1) = 6/6 = 1.0. C2 is 'Mat the cat is on a there.' which has all its 2-grams not appearing in the reference, resulting in BLEU**1(C2) = 7/7 = 1.0 and BLEU**2(C2)]

159
nComputing n-grams precisionlA way to get higher scores for well-formed sentences is to consider matching 2-grams or 3-grams instead of 1-grams only. Let’s call BLEU**₁ the score that considers only 1-grams and BLEU**₂ the score that considers only 2-grams.lExample:•R: There is a cat on the mat. •C1: There is a cat on the mat. •C2: Mat the cat is on a there.•C1 has six 2-grams and they all appear on the reference R, thus: »BLEU**₁(C1) = 7/7 = 1.0 & BLEU**₂(C1) = 6/6 = 1.0 •Instead, in C2 all the 2-grams don’t appear in the reference, thus: »BLEU**₁(C2) = 7/7 = 1.0 & BLEU**₂(C2) = 0/6 = 0.0
BLEU (2/4)

The second bullet point is about penalizing short candidate translations, providing an example where two sentences are compared. The example includes a reference sentence, a candidate sentence, and the calculated BLEU scores for both, which are equal to 1.0. nIn the literature, it has been found that the geometric mean of the BLEU**ₙ scores with n between one and four has the best correlation with human evaluation, therefore it’s the score more commonly adopted, called MEAN_BLEU**.nPenalizing short candidate translationslConsider•R: There is a cat on the mat. •C: There is a cat. •The scores are: BLEU**₁(C) = 4/4 = 1.0 & BLEU**₂(C) = 3/3= 1.0 •Looks like C achieves a perfect BLEU**ₙ score for each n, even though the candidate is missing a piece of text with respect to the reference.

doc.page_content: [Image caption: The slide is titled 'BLEU (4/4)' and is the fourth in a series. It discusses the Brevity Penalty (BP) which is used to penalize a candidate whose length is less than the reference. The slide provides a formula for the final BLEU score, which is BLEU = BP * MEAN_BLEU**. The BP is further defined by two conditions: if 'c' is greater than 'r', BP is 1, and if 'c' is less than or equal to 'r', BP is calculated using the formula e^(1-r/c). Here, 'c' represents the length of the candidate, and 'r' represents the length of the reference. The slide is numbered 161.]

161
nBrevity Penalty (BP)lTo add a penalty for a candidate whose length is less than the referencenTherefore, the final BLEU score is:                         BLEU = BP * MEAN_BLEU**
BLEU (4/4)
c:the length of the candidate r: the length of the reference

doc.page_content: [Image caption: Slide 162 contains text in bullet points. The header reads 'BERTScore.' Below the header, there are two bullet points explaining the concept of BERTScore as a method used to measure the quality of text summarization. It states that BERTScore measures the similarity of the text summary to the original text. The second bullet point lists two common issues that n-gram-based metrics often encounter, which BERTScore addresses. The issues are that n-gram models can incorrectly match paraphrases due to semantically accurate expressions differing from the reference text's surface form, and that they cannot capture long-range dependencies or penalize semantically significant reordering. The slide has a white background with text in black and blue.]

162
nBertScore is a method used to measure the quality of text summarization. This method measures how similar the text summary is to the original text.nBertScore addresses two common issues that n-gram-based metrics often encounter.lFirst, n-gram models tend to incorrectly match paraphrases because semantically accurate expressions may differ from the surface form of the reference text, which can lead to incorrect performance estimation. lSecond, n-gram models cannot capture long-range dependencies and penalize semantically significant reordering.

nBERTScore ArchitectureBERTScore
Step 3: Token Matching for Precision and Recall: Each token in the candidate sentence is matched to the most similar token in the reference sentence, and vice versa, to compute Recall and Precision, which are then combined to calculate the F1 score.

nTo fine-tune LLM with reinforcement learning, we can view this problem by MDP, which is described as a tuple (S, A, P, R, γ, d0):lState space S: the space of input token sequences: •q ∪ {o0, o1, . . . , ot}. •s0 is defined as the token sequence of question q.lAction space A: the space of tokens ot.lReward function R(q, {o0, . . . , ot}): a score that reflects the quality of the generated answer to the question.lTransition P: st+1 = st ∪ ot+1.lInitial distribution d0: the distribution of question q.

172
nRLC uses two types of prompts for evaluating the self-generated text: l(1) Correctness Evaluation Prompt (CEP)•"Is the answer to the question correct? The question is: [Q]. The answer is: [A]”•The reward is a 0-1 value based on the evaluation result.l(2) Quality Evaluation Prompt (QEP)•"Please evaluate the answer to the question and give me an evaluation score from 1 to 10. The question is: [Q]. The answer is [A]".

nReinforcement learning is an AI area concerned with how an intelligent agent ought to take actions in a dynamic environment in order to maximize the cumulative reward. nDifferent from supervised learning in not needing labelled input/output pairs to be presented, reinforcement learning focuses on finding a balance between exploration (of uncharted territory) and exploitation (of current knowledge) with the goal of maximizing the long term reward.
